<p>'l'l.1is n&gt;l~tllH' is one of a 6~volume final report of th.e_ Study of a Single-·stage Earth-
1~rhital 'iku::;able Vehicle (SEHV). The study was conducted •by the Chrysler Corpoi:a-:-:· 
t ion ::;pace Division ( CC8D) for the National A~rohautics and Space Administration, 
</p>
<p>. Gc•or~l' C. l\1a1:shall Spnct' Flight Center under Contract NAS8;_26341. The p1.1rpose 
of tlll''::;tully was lo evaluate the potential ofSERV as.the boostelementoLa ca'ndidat9 
spacl' transportation system. To establish the SE.RV.potential, five key technical 
a1·eas affecting concept feasibility were identified for exa_mination_: engine_performGtnce, 
acirodyna m ic characteristics, thermal protection·; subsystem weights, .and the la11tli11g 
method. The results of these-analyses are published· in a final report consisting of the 
following six volumes: 
</p>
<p>Volume 1 Summary 
Volume 2 Aerodynamic Model Testing 
Volume 3 Concept Evaluation 
Volume 4 Vehicle Definition 
Volume G Operations Definition 
Volume G Resources 
</p>

well Corporation, Rocketdyne Division, who under subcontract assisted in the model 
test; and analyzed the test results of the uniquely integrated SERV engine-to-structure 
concept. Rocketdyne also generated parametric engine data and designed the SERV 
aerospike engine. Chrysler also acknowledges the support and technical assista11ce 
received from Detroit Diesel Allison Division of General Motors Corporation vvho pro­
vided parametric engine data for advanced technology direct lift gas turbine engines arid 
the AVCO Systems Division who provided design and cost data for thermal protection 
systems. In addition, acknowledgement is made to the following NASA and DOD agencies

and AF-AEDC.

<p>. . . 
removal of aerospike engine protection doors. A lead cell was used to tneaa'ure 
the net vehicle axial force and extensive pressure measurements,with selected 
temperature measur.ements were made. 
</p>
<p>A pilot test was conducted in the AEDC l-foot PWT to ,.investigate the best model 
support mounting technique to be used in the 16TPWT base flow test. This_ test 
</p>
<p>. utilized a 0.156 percent scale model simulating-the test conditions at which 
the larger scale test was to be conducted. The effect on base. pressure of for­
ward strut-sting mounting systems was compared to the effect produced by aft 
sting mounting systems.. From this test, it was concluded that a forward floor.!. 
"mounted strut-sting mounting system could be advantageously employed 'with a · 
minimum influence on base pressure magnitudes and symmetry. 
</p>
<p>I 
</p>
<p>Scale model fo.r,ce, .. tests were conducJ:.ed -.in the ,NASA .Ames 6~f,oot Sup~rso.nic Wind. 
Tunnel (SWT) and the Langley Unitary Plan-Wind Tunnel (UPWT) to defirie the aero­
dynamic flight performance characteristics of both the ascent and descent vehic• 
les, These were to determine the aerodynamic stability characteristics, normal 
force, axial force, and pitching moment through the complete flight Mach number. 
range of 0,4 to 4.64. No testing··o,f the descent-vehicle above Mach 4.64 was 
conducted as it was. consider.ed adequate .for Task 4 ·vehicle_ definition require­
ments to extrapolate the higher supersonic Mach number data to the reentry 
hypersonic Mach range by application of reference Apollo data and hypersonic 
flow theory. 
</p>
<p>In the ascent-portion of these tests, 0.55 percent scale models of the SERV 
baseline configuration with the various payload options were utilized. · The 
models were approximately 6 inches in maximum cross-section diameter. Tests 
of the descent configuration utilized similar 0.55 percent scale models of a 
maximum diameter of 6 inches in the heat-shield-first reentry and decelera.tion 
attitude. The influence of descent configuration geometric parameters such ~s 
removal of engine doors, afterbody geometry, heat shield corner radius, and using 
the aerospike engine protection doors as flaps were investigated. Aerodynamic 
force characteristics of the ascent configurations were tested through an angle .. 
of-attack range of -4 to +16 degrees, while the descent configurations were 
tested from +4 to -50 degrees in the subsonic and transonic range (utilizing 
offset stings), and from -6 to -34 degrees angle-of-attack range in the higher 
supersonic regime • 
</p>
<p>Figure 1-2 presents the test conditions of Reynolds number versus Mach number 
for all aerodynamic tests conducted, with a comparison of the full scale flight 
Reynolds number for a typical trajectory. As is so often the case, all tests 
were conducted at simulated Reynolds numbers well below the full scale values.

<p>Section 2

<p>A$·.CENl BASE FLOWlEST 
</p>
<p>2. 1 PILOT MODEL TEST 
</p>
<p>. The objective '.Of thia test .was to determine experimentally the...effect on base. 
pressures of various model mounting ... supports .and nose shapes .of a 0.156 percent 
scale model of the SERV ~scent configuration, in the transonic region. The re­
sults of this test were used_ in _determining the mounting of the 2.5 percen,t 
cold-flow-model of SERV in theAEDC·l6T PWT~ . The 0.156 ·percen,t SQale model test 
was .conducted in the AEDC. 1.-foot PWT (reference 12). Sketches: of the configura­
tions teated.a.re shown in figures.2.1-Lthrough·2.l-4. 
</p>
<p>The model was designed by Chrysler and ;.,under subcontract, was fabric;:lt:ed from 
aluminum and stainless. steel by.Microcraft, Inc. of Tullahoma, Terines-s·ee-•. Ten 
orifices were located on the model, 6 static orifices in the vertical centerline 
of the model base and 4 flow orifices on the shoulder of the :forebody, ;each 90 
degrees apart (figures 2.1~3 and 2.1-5). 
</p>
<p>2.1.1 TEST PROCEDURE 
</p>
<p>The model was mounted in the test se_ction by two methods ,sting mounted from the 
base of the model and strut mounted from the nose of the model. With the model 
sting mounted, three nose shapes were tested; a large diameter payload of cargo·· 
module, an extended personnel module, and an extended nose. With the model 
</p>
<p>_strut-mounted, two forward sting lengths were tested, one was a length that 
· locat«.;d the base of the model 1. 70 strut chord lengths (3 .03 inches) from the 
tailing edge of the strut and the other located the base of the model 3.74 
strut chord lengths (6.66 inches) from the trailing. edge of the strut •. The 
strut was mounted to the side wall of the test section. Photographs of the 
model, strut mounted and sting mounted, are shown in figures 2.1•6 and 2.1-7. 
Testing was perfornied at angles of attack of O, -5 and -10 degrees from Mach 
numbers of 0.6 to 1.30. A detailed description of the test plan is presented 
in reference 1. 
</p>
<p>Several problems developed during testing. Due to the limited space in the 
model, it was nearly impossible to keep the flexible tubing which connected 
the pressure orifices to the pressure transducers from kinking inside .the model 
with the result that several orifices were inoperative. With the model strut 
mounted, the tunnel had to be opened and part of the tunnel wall removed in 
order to change the angle-of-attack, resulting in a lengthy downtime and there­
fore limiting the angle-of-attack data obtained. The model was not mounted in 
front of the tunnel window so shadowgraphs and/or Schieren pictures of flow· 
characteristics could not be obtained. 
</p>

<p>2 .1. 2 RESULTS .AND DISCUSSION 
*~ 
</p>
<p>Only pressure data were obtained from this test, and figure 2. f .... a present. the 
base pressure ratio (PbVPoo) plotted versus radial diameters (R/D) at zero angle• 
of-attack for the various configurations tested •. These data show that in the 
</p>
<p>·transonic region, the long nose sting on the st:rut-lllOunted modei had less in­
fluence on the model base pressures than the shorter nose sting .. with the model 
sting-mounted. from the base, the length of the nose had rto effect ·on&gt;the pa$e 
pressures. 'The short ·nose sting was tested with the model both strut and ,sting 
mounted and the data showed slightly less effect on the base pressures with the 
mode 1 st:i,ng mounted. Figure 2 .1-9 presents press.ure coefficient data (Cp) ob­
tained on the forebody with the model stl:'ut mounted. These were the only fore-
</p>
<p>. body pressure data obtained. during the test. .The pressures on the fore body were 
more positive with the short nose sting than with the long. nqse. sting.·. Complete.• 
results.and analysis of the wind tunnel test are presented in refer.ence·2. 
</p>
<p>The m~thod selected to mount the .2 .5 percent cold-flow scale ~.del in the 
AEDC 16T PWT was the strut mount with the long nose sting. The strut mounting 
</p>
<p>· was selected because with the sting mounted in the base and with aerospike en­
gine .exhaust out the base, the flow would impinge on the sting and.,,even the 
smallest sting could give erroneous results. Also, due to the requirement of 
routing the air supply into the model, the sting would have been: .. of · a,diameter 
that could have resulted in large base pressure errors. Because the differences 
in the effect on base pressures between the front strut mount and •the aft sting 
mount were small, and because the long nose sting h,ad less effect on the base 
pressures than the short nose sting, it was decided that the front stru't mount 
with the long nose sting would have the least et'fect on the base flow character­
istics. 
</p>
<p>2.2 COLD-FLOW MODEL TEST 
</p>
<p>In order to adequately define the aerospike engine performance and resulting 
net vehicle axial forces during ascent flight, it was necessary to determine 
the flow characteristics of the engine with its large-area-ratio at open wake 
operating pressure ratio under the influence of a simulated flight slipstream 
over ·the bhmt fore body configuration. Small-scale, cold-flow engine~model 
wind tunnel slipstream testing had proven to be an inexpensive and accurate 
means of verifying these characteristics on past programs as discussed in 
appendi:,c A. Cold-flow engine wind tunnel tests, under well controlied simu­
lati.on conditions, had been used to establish the flow characteristics and 
performance trends of typical aerospike nozzles opera~ing in a slipstream. 
The experience gained by the reference testing, noted in appendix A, provided 
valuable guidelines for defining the necessary model scaling and testing tech­
niques. 
</p>
<p>A test program was conducted in the AEDC 16T PWT from December 3 through.Decem­
ber 8, 1970, utilizing a 2.5 percent scale model of the SERV baseline geometry, 
incorporating a high expansion ratio, short length aerospike engine, The test 
was conducted in still air and in a slipstream simulating flight altitude con­
ditions and freestream Mach numbers from Oto 1.25. These tests did not dupli­
cate completely the pressure ratios of an actual trajectory. The resultant 
data were to establish sufficient magnitudes and trends to extrapolate analy-tic­
ally to the higher pressure ratio flight conditions. Reference 3 presents a. 
complete pretest plan for this program and references 4 through 7 and appendix A 
</p>

<p>2. 2. l TEST FACILITY 
</p>
<p>The AEDC 16T PWT is a closed-circt1it, continuous-flow t,.mnel capable o.f .being 
operated at Mach numbers from O. 20 to L.60 •. The test section is 16 by 16 feet 
in cross section and 40 feet long. The ·tunnel can be operated at stagnation 
pressures down to .120 psfa at specific Mach numbers. Perforated :walls in the 
test section allow continuous operat.ion •through the Mach number range with a 
minimum of wall interfii•r~nce. A more detailed descriptfon.;.of this test facility 
:j.s presented in reference 12. 
</p>
<p>2.2.2 MODEL DESIGN AND FABRICATION.· 
\ 
</p>
<p>The test model was a 2. 5 .percent scale replica of the SERV baseline ascent ve­
hicle. It had a physical diameter of approximately 27 inches at the base a:nd 
was approx:i.mateJ.y 20 inches in length, A 417 area ratio aerospike e1Jgine, de.­
signed tc use air as .a test gas, was. incor,porated.~ The model was mount:ed on 
a fore body sting 126. 5 in. long which was strut wounted .to· the. tunne 1 floor. 
Aerospike engine the.rm.al protection doors we-re a,t:.tached to the outer perimeter 
of the aerospike engine nozzle flange. The model was instrumented with an ax­
ial force load cell, 163 static pre~sure taps, and .4 thE!rmocouple gages. -Ref­
erences 3 and 4, and append.ix A present a more d.etailed de·scription .of t~e test 
</p>
<p>. model and instrumentation. Figures 2 ,2,;.l and .2 ~•2·-2, illustta.te the inodel' geom• 
etry, instrumentation, and installation in the AEDC-test facility. 
</p>
<p>The model was designed jointly by Chrysler and Rocketdyne and fabrica:ted under 
subcontract to Rocketdyne. Final assembly was completed on November.4, 1970 
and operational checkout was conducted in the Rocketdyne Rocket Nozzle Test 
Facility in Los Angeles, California, through November 10, 1970. 
</p>
<p>2.2.3 TEST PROCEDURE 
</p>
<p>The complet~ test plan followed in this program is published in reference 3. 
The model was tested with and without engine flow at freestream Mach numbers 
of 0, 0.6, 0.8, 0.9, 1.1, an:d 1.25. With en,gine flow, model chamber pressure 
was maintained at a nominal value of 400 psia; Tunnel pressure was varied to 
obtain a range of aerospike engine. chamber pressure to freestream static press­
ure ratios at each Mach number. ·At each test condition, data were recorded 
with no engine flow, with primary engine flow, and with primary engine plus 
simulate.cl secondary turbine exhaust flow. As mentioned previously, the altitude 
versus Mach-number test conditions did not duplicate completely the full-scale 
trajectory. A comparison of the reference trajectory to test conditions is 
presented in figure 2.2-3. 
</p>
<p>There were three major problems incurred with this test_progra:m. First the 
axial force load cell readings indicated a significant bias due to the thermal 
stresses in the capillary spoke lines used to feed the high-pressure air from 
the center pipe to the outer combustion chamber annulus. This bias was a small 
percentage of net· thrust, but it was especially significant when measuring 
engine-off axial force or drag, due to the very low tunnel freestream densities. 
This problem was solved by running a Mach number sweep at high tunnel total· 
pressures to measure model axial forces without engine on. These data were then 
normalized to a drag coefficient to be used in the data reduction for engine 
thrust at the low tunnel density test conditions. 
</p>

<p>It became apparent after seveia,l ~ttempts that it would be VE,!ry difficult to 
hold the test section Mach nt.iinber condition of 1.05 as required by the test 
plan. Thet'efore, a test Mach number of 1,1 was run in the place of 1.05. 
</p>
<p>In the last phases of testing a p;ressure seal in the facility started leaking;· 
this made it impossible to achieve the minimum test section·total pressure of 
120 psfa. · This resulted in the reductio.n of the doors-o,ff al.t·itude simulation 
for the final phases of. testing. 
</p>
<p>With the exception of these difficulties, the test pro.gram was compl:eted with 
excellent results. The ,tes·t model functi'oned Mi'fhout ;failure ,throughout the 
complete program. Data acquHdtion was accomplished with a minimum of repeat 
conditions required. Schlieren photographs of the .model base• flow are prese,:nted 
in figure. 2 .2~4 at specific fie~stream Mach number and ,engine pressure ratio 
test conditions.. The engine exhaust plume. can be easily distinguished; however, 
the s·lipstrean1 ambient flow is not visible due to its very low density for these 
test conditions (noted·above). 
</p>
<p>...... ~, 
</p>
<p>2. 2. 4 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION 
</p>
<p>Complete listings of the-test data in digitized.form and graphic presentations 
have been published in references 5 through 7 •. Appendix A :by: Ro.cketc:iyne. docu­
ments their analysis of the engine;performance test dat;:a: with' thE! metho.d···of 
extrapolation to full sea.le. · 
</p>
<p>It became apparent after preliminary analysis of test data that the engine jet 
exhaust impinged ori the engine protection doors for engine pressure ratios, 
Pc/Poo, of.greater than 380. This had a tendency to alter engine performance, 
due to a high buildup in the base pressure. As impingement on the engine doors 
represented an intolerable design condition with respect to door loads and· heat- · . ..,. 
ing, the engine data with do.ors removed were used to evaluate engine performance 
and an incremental drag term was included for attachment of the engine doors for 
flight performance evaluation as discussed in volume 4. 
</p>
<p>The axial load cell measurements with engine on were used with the evaluated 
forebody drag and specific local surface pressure measurements to calculate 
net engine model thrust at each freestream Mach number and engine pressure 
ratio condit.iort. Figure 2 .2-5 presents these results in thrust coefficient 
form as a function of engine pressure ratio. There is a definite reduction in 
engine thrust performance with in.creasing freestream Mach number. This is typ­
ical of the influence of the blunt body base pressure trends which this vehicle 
should exhibit.. The effect of th~ ambient slipstream of aerospike engine per­
formance is determined by the expansion of the engine exhaust flow into the base. 
For all flight operating pressure ratios, the noz.zle momentum thrust term and 
exhaust flow compression on the aerospike ramp are constant. 0nly_expansion of 
the flow from the end of the ramp into the base region changes with altitude and 
slipstream effects. The low expansion pressures of the ambient flow expanding 
over the peripheral base geometry of the vehicle are reflected. through the ex­
haust plume into the base region caus.ing a reduction in the effective pressure 
acting on the base heat shield. As this region is considered a component of the 
total engine thrust, there is, there·fore, a reduction in the engine thrust per-
formance as compared to the condition of no slipstream (M. = 0) • · 
</p>

<p>Engine thrust perfor~nce increases with increasing pressure ratio and should 
increase with the addition of injection of turbine exhaust or secondary flow 
into the base. The increase in engine'performance by secondary·flow. injection 
will be a small percentage, but should- definitely be distinguishable. That was 
</p>
<p>"q\i, not always the result in this test; however, the turbine exhaust location and 
f flow rate were fixed and no attempt was made at optimization. 
</p>
<p>4if'!\, (:f;ff;i•;, 
~'../' 
</p>
<p>Figure 2 .2-6(a) shows mean base pressure ratio (PB-c./P00 ) over the area outside 
of the engine nozzle perimeter. · These pressure ratios are a direct 1:unction of 
the pumping action of the engine exhaust flow and the ambient slipstream expand­
ing over the vehicle externa 1 geometry. They are seen to decrease w:Vth increas• 
ing slipstream Mach number, but have little- variation (except M:::::0.6) with engine 
pressure ratio (Pc/P 00 ) for the range tested. At M =O.O the drop in this 
</p>
<p>· local pressure below ambient must be·.d.ue solely to the pumping action of the 
engine; , · · 
</p>
<p>The mean pressure ratios (PBE/Poo) in the base heat shield region are shown 
in figure 2 .2-6(b) as functions of· flight Ma.ch number and engine pressure· 
ratio (Pc/Pco ). The_se too decrease with increasing freestream Hach number 
and reflect almost directly the reduction in engine thrust performance due 
to slipstream effects. Wtthin th&amp;,pressure ratio range tested, these pres~ 
sures are. also very nearly constant wtt-h increasing en·gine pressure ratio 
(Pc/P 00 ). Secondary flow- injection into· the, base definitely appears to iq­
crease the pressures in this region. 
</p>
<p>2.2.5 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>The most significant result of the SERV base flow test was that the engine 
.exhaust jet and base flow field were typically characteristic of aerospike 
engines. The integrated SERV concept,with large area ratio at open wake and 
low engine pressure ratios, did not indicate any new or different trends from 
previous reference test data. Engine performance did prove to be sensitive to 
forebody geometry slipstream and the exhaust plume expanding into the base area. 
This of course was due to the large portion of engine thrust which is determined 
by these areas for SERV. The necessary data were acquired, however, to assess 
the influence of all these parameters for the specific SERV vehicle application, 
within the accuracy level of a Phase A feasibility study. The basis for extra­
polation to full-scale flight performance was established and the comparison 
with original predictions was good '(see appendix A for detailed analysis and 
discussion). · 
</p>

<p>Section 3 
</p>
<p>ASCENT CONFIGURATION FORCE TESTS . 
</p>
<p>3.1 TEST PLAN 
</p>
<p>. To verify SERV vehicle payload, .c·apa·bility and flight control requirements in 
the ascent mode, a -definition of flight aerodynamic stability and drag charac .. 
terist:t~s was obtained from wind tunnel tests according to plans outli.ned in 
references 8 and 9. These experimental force tests conducted in the 6,-foot 
Supersonic. Wind TunneL (SWT). and .. the Unitary Plan Wind Tunnel (UPWT) at NASA 
Ames Research Center and Langley Research Center, res.pectively; covered a 
combined Mach number range. of 0.4· to· 4.6!~ .(subsonic _to high supersonic}. 
Lower Mach numbers of 0.4 to 2.0 were obtained in the Ames facil;i.ty, while 
remaining supersonic s.peeds .were acquired in .the :Langley facility. Angl.e,- .. 
of-attack range for ascent mode testing varied from ..;._4 to +16 degrees in 
both facilities. 
</p>
<p>Ascent configurations tested in these programs varied primarily· in payload 
geometry with four different forebodies being considered. These were: Re­
tracted Personnel Module (PM); Winged Spacecraft (MURP); Large Payload (LPL); 
and Extended Personnel Module (PM). An additional variation involved a 
change in degree of aerospike protection door openitig. 
</p>
<p>3.2 MODEL DESIGN AND FABRICATION 
</p>
<p>The four ascent configurations having 25-degree aerospike engine door openings 
are illustrated in figure 3.2-1. Figure 3.2-l(e) shows the Extended Personnel 
Module configuration'again but with engine doors open 45 degrees. The models 
were fabricated of stainless steel to0.55 percent of full scale by Standard 
Tool and Die Inc. of Los Angeles, California. All were approximately 6 inches 
in diameter with the largest. (LPL) having an overall length of 8.87 inches and 
the shortest (Retracted Personnel Module) ,5.69 inches. All component model 
parts (including interchangeable reentry configuration parts) are illustrated 
in figure 3.2-2. 
</p>
<p>Pertinent model reference dimensions and areas for ascent con~igurations were: 
reference diameter of 6 inches; reference area of 28.26 ~quare inches; and a 
base area of 21.23 square inches. 
</p>
<p>Model strengths were adequate to withstand anticipated maximum loads with a 
safety fact©r of four (4.0) based on material yield strength. 
</p>
<p>3 .3 TEST. PROCEDURE° 
</p>
<p>The tunnel installation, typical in both facilities, is illustrated in figure 
3.3-1. The models were sting mounted from a rear tunnel support system which

<p>incorporated a Langley UT-22 .stra,in .gage balance and tubing for base cavity

loads of 300 pounds in normal and axial directions and 300 inch pounds of

than these limits •

force, moment and base pressure coefficients defined as:

3) CA = qooS 
</p>
<p>C = 4) AB 
</p>
<p>(P00 _(Pbl +Pbz) /2)Ab 
</p>
<p>qoos 
</p>
<p>where q 00 = test section dynamic pressure, S = reference area, n= reference 
diameter, and Ab= ascent configuration base area. 
</p>
<p>Moments measured by the balance were initially referenced to its electrical 
center, and then transferred to model stationO.O. (figure 3.2-1). The transfer 
distance from the balance moment reference to the reference model station was 
measured by hanging weights with the balance installed. In addition to the 
above coefficients, Schlieren photographs of the shock wave systems were acquir­
ed at all conditions of interest. 
</p>
<p>Test run numbers, correlated with nominal Mach numbers and angle-of-attack 
__ ranges used in the testing, are shown in matrix form in table 3.3-1. Angle-
</p>
<p>. of-attack ranges were generally covered in 2-degree steps. 
</p>
<p>No specific problems developed during the testing of ascent configurations. 
No significant bias or shift in the data was seen except in the pitching moment 
characteristics at Mach 0.4 for both MURP and Retracted PM configurations, more 
so for the latter model, however. This data bias was more of a serious problem 
in the reentry portion of the testing where offset stings were employed to 
achieve extended angle'!"of-attack ranges. In these cases, the magnitude of any 
data shift was often smaller than the accuracy limits of the balance. 
</p>
<p>3 .4 DISCUSSION OF RESULTS 
</p>
<p>The results of these experimental investigations of SERV ascent configurations 
have been presented in detail in data reports (references 10 and 11). Aero­
dynamic characteristics given in these references were generally in.agreement 
with predicted trends as estimated and derived by empirical and/or theoretical 
methods. Some sample characteristics from references 10 and 11 are shown in 
coefficient farm in figures 3.4-1 and 3.4-2 as functions of angle-of-attack for 
a Mach number of 1.2 (peak dynamic pressure region) and a second at a high 
supersonic value of 4.64. 
</p>

<p>3 .4 .1 PAYLOAD VARIATION 
</p>
<p>Effects of payload variation at Mach numbers of 1.2 and 4 .64 are pre1ented. in 
figures 3 .4-1 and 3·.4-2. At Mach 1.2, normal force (CN) and axial force (CA &amp; 
CA ) , coefficients exhibit trends· for the various configurations that do not 
diifer appreciably. The maximum ob.served deviations occur in the axial force 
in the ±2° angle-of-attack range~ The effect of payload on pitching moment, 
however, at thif3 transonic Mach number (figure 3.4-l(b)) is quite significant 
(beyond a =2°). Differences due to payload at the higher Mach number are quite 
noticeable for all characteristics shown. Normal force and pitching moment 
coefficient magnitudes are generally highest for the configurations having the 
longer and wider forebodies. Conversely,·this same class of vehicles produces 
minimum axial forces. In all characteristics shown her~, the Retracted PM 
and MURP forebodies show similar levels .of magnitude, indicating a small effect 
due to the more· sle.nder· forebody of· the MURP configuration. 
</p>
<p>Dragcoeffi~ients (ata = o0) from references 10 and 11 have been plotted in 
figure 3.4-3 for the entire combined Mach number range of 0.4 to 5.0. In this 
figure, the base drag .coefficient shown results from t.C;!Sting involving no sim­
ulation of engine operation and is constant for all configurations. It has no 
relationship to the power-on base pressure effects reported elsewhere. The 
effect of payload shape on these drag characteristics (figure 3.4-3) becomes 
significant beyond Mach 1.0 where divergences really begin, with the Retracted 
PM configuration (figure 3.2-l(a)) exhibiting the highest level, and the LPL 
configuration (figure 3.2-l(c)) showing the lowest level. While the Extended 
PM configuration (figure 3.2-l(d)) is not included in figure 3.4-3, it would 
show characteristics intermediate to LPL and MURP payload vehicles. · (Total 
drag coefficient for this vehicle is presented in figure 3.4-6 in another 
comparison.) 
</p>
<p>Schlieren photographs (figures 3.4-4 and 3.4-5) from the Ames and Langley test 
facilities represent the flow characteristics about the various ascent config­
urations at the sample Mach numbers of 1.2 and 4.64. The previously discussed 
drag characteristics of the various vehicles may be correlated with these shock 
formations to some degree. At Mach 1.2, drag divergence due to payload has not 
yet occurred, so that little difference in the bow shock wave formations of the 
various payloads should be apparent. Examination of the sequence of Mach 1.2 
pictures in figure 3.4-4 indicates this is true; however, the LPL configuration 
is beginning to display more obliqueness and a tendency toward the conical shock 
pattern associated with reduced drag (figure 3.4-4(c)). At the higher Mach 
number of 4.64, shock formations seen in figure 3.4-5 reflect the differences 
in drag seen in figure 3.4-3. The progression of the· bow shock system from the 
near normal shock case of the short-length Retracted PM forebody to the increas­
ingly more conical shock system of the longer nose payloads seen in the sequence 
of pictures (3.4-5(a) through 3.4-5(d)) corresponds to .the regression in drag 
due to forebody shape seen in figure 3.4-3. 
</p>
<p>3.4.2 ENGINE DOOR OPENING 
</p>
<p>With the exception of some minor effect on the normal force at lower Mach num­
bers and higher angles-of-attack, an increase of engine door opening from 25. 
degrees·to 45 degrees affects the base axial force primarily. The increase of 
about 25·percent in this parameter due to the increased opening, also increases 
the total drag coefficient by the differential between curves seen in figure 
3.4-6 where dof)r opening cases are compared. 
</p>

<p>3.4,3 STATIC STABILITY C.· 
\ ·1, ' .,, t' ,,:. ~-- .-· 
</p>
<p>The slope of normal force and pitching moment coefficient curves in the linear 
range of angle of attack (±4° about zero), provide criteria which, when related 
properly, indicate the center of pressure location from the vehicle base and 
provide overall knowledge of vehicle stability. These characteristics are pre­
sented in figure 3.4-7. 
</p>
<p>Center of pressure locations shown in figure 3.4-7 agree generally with the 
predicted levels, With most payloads exhibiting marginal ·static stability 
characteristics in the range of 0.2 ·to 0.4 calibers forward of the vehicle 
base. Up to approximately Mach 1.5, normal force gradiemt (CNa:). trends £or 
all payload. versions were somewhat similar; beyond this Mach.number, ·however, 
trends were different from predicted .with wide·· divergencies noted,. 
</p>
<p>3. 5 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>Conclusions were summarized in the·following paragraphs. 
</p>
<p>Experimentally acquired data were generally in agreement with predicted trends. 
</p>
<p>At Mach numbers below 1.2, payload.variation did not appreciably af.foct normal 
and axial force characteristics •. At angles of attack greater than 2 degrees, 
the effect on pitching moment was quite significant. 
</p>
<p>At higher Mach numbers, payload shape has a significant effect on all aerody~ 
.·· namic characteristics. The longer, larger-diameter payload shapes produce the 
highest normal forces and pitchin@: moments. but produce minimum axial forces. 
The primary effect of a change in the opening of the aerospike engine doors 
from 25 degrees to 45 degrees is about a 25 percent increase in the engine-off 
base drag. 
</p>
<p>All payload shapes exhibit marginal static stability characteristics with 
center of pressure locations in the order of 0.2 to 0.4 calibers forward of 
the base. 
</p>

<p>Section 4 
</p>
<p>DESCENT CONFIGURATION FORCE TESTS I 
</p>
<p>Descent performance of the SERV concept is contingent upon reentry aerodynamic 
drag, lift--to-drag ratio,, and ·trim stability. In the lower supersonic through-.. 
subsonic -phase. of flight, vehicle o ta tic aerodynamic· stability0 '~h'a.racteristics ·· 
are very important factors in minimized control system requirements. In order· 
to determine these aerodynamic. performance characteristics to the accuracy re­
quired to prove feasibility, wind tunnel force tests were conducted on paramet~. 
ric scale models of the SERVdescent configuration. 
</p>
<p>These tests were conducted in the NASA Ames Research Center 6-foot SWT and 
Langley Research Genter UPWT over. a combined Mach number range ·of.,· from O .4 .. to :', 
4.64.' The higher Mach riumber data were used to extrapolate the performance 
characteristics to hypersonic Mach numbers through the use of applicable Apollo 
reference data and hypersonic flow theory. Geometric effects such as heat shield 
corner radius, afterbody configuration, removal of aerospike engine protection 
doors, and opening engine doors in the lower Mach range, were investigated. 
</p>
<p>4.1 TEST PLAN 
</p>
<p>Aerodynamic wind tunnel force. tests were conducted using 0.55 percent scale 
models of the SERV descent .baseline concept with specific geo~etric variations. 
The NASA Ames Research Center 6-foot SWT was utilized for the test .Mach number 
range from 0.4 to 2.0. The Langley Research Center UPWT was used for the super­
sonic test Mach numbers 2.6 to 4.64.· The higher Mach number t~sts were conduct­
ed for an angle-of-attack range of -6 to -34 degrees (heat-shield-down attitude) 
while the lower supersonic, transonic and subsonic tests were conducted at angles 
of attack from +4 to -50 degrees. A 3-comp&lt;&gt;nent force balance was used to meas­
ure the aerodynamic parameters, normal force (CN), axial force (CA) and pitching 
moment (CM). An offset sting mounting system was employed to achieve .the com­
plete angle-of-attack ranges. References 8 and 9 document in detail the original 
pretest plans. Both the Ames and Langley test facilities are continuous flow · 
tunnels with the capability of accurate testing of this size model within their 
respective operating Mach ranges. The test Reynold's· number conditions are de­
fined by figure 1-2. References 13 and 14 provide detail~d definition of the 
tunnel capabilities and operating systems. 
</p>
<p>4.2 MODEL DESIGN AND FABRICATION 
</p>
<p>The descent force models were 0.55 percent replicas of the SERV descent base­
line configuration with parametric variations as illustrated i.n figure 4.2-L 
The models were designed by Chrysler and fabricated under purchase order by 
Standard Tool and Die, Company of Los Angeles, California. 
</p>
<p>4-1 
</p>

</p>
<p>The models were approximately 6 inches in maximum diameter. Three heat shield 
corner radii combinations were tested with two different afterbody configurations. 
The baseline reentry vehicle was tested with and without engine doors. Stress 
analysis was performed to verify that the models, force balance, and support sys­
tem would withstand the maximum anttcipated loads with a safety factor· of ·four 
(4.0) based on the material yield strength. 
</p>
<p>4.3 TEST PROCEDURE 
</p>
<p>Wind tunnel installation typical of both tes~ facilities is illustrated in 
figure 4.3-1. The models were aft mounted utilizing offset stings and canted 
models to achieve the complete angle-of-attack range. The Langley-Research· 
</p>
<p>- Center UT-22 3-component strain gage· balance was··used· 0·to&gt;rneasure. the·· forces- · 
and moments, Base flow interference of the offset stings and low input loads 
to the balance (capability of 300 lb norma.l and axial load, and 300 in./lb of 
moment), resulted in bias errors at·small angle of attack near zero load, How­
ev_er, verification in the accuracy of the higher angle-of-attack data allowed 
the data t.o be shifted and corrected for these biases within a reasonable toler­
ance. 
</p>
<p>Data obtained from the UT-22 balance and pressure transducers were reduced tb 
force, moment and base pressure coefficients as defined in subsection 3,3. Mo­
ments measured by the balance were initially referenced to its electrical c;:en­
ter, and then transferred to model station O.O. (See figur·e 4.2-L) The trans­
fer distance from the balance moment reference to the reference model stations 
was measured by hanging weights with the balance installed. In addition to the 
above coefficients, Schlieren photographs of the sh6ck wave systems were Be­
quired at a 11 conditions of interest. 
</p>
<p>No significant problems developed during these tests,_other than the low angle 
of attack low load range bias due to the use of the offset sting system. These 
biases existed only in the subsonic and transonic Mach ranges where base pres­
sures were significantly affected. Above Mach 1.5, these biases tended to dis­
sipate. 
</p>
<p>4.4 DISCUSSION OF RESULTS 
</p>
<p>The results of these wind.tunher tests have been presented in detail in ref­
erences 10 and 11. Aerodynamic characteristics of the SERV baseline descent 
vehicle were in agreement with previous Apollo test data.· The configuration 
data exhibited sufficient lift-to-drag ratio (L/D) values at angle of attack 
(approximately 0.3 at a= -22°, as predicted) and also indicated that the ve-
hicle would have adequate stability margins through transonic and subsonic de­
celeration flight. Normal force, axial force, pitching moment coefficients, 
and lift-to-drag ratio (L/D) are presented at representative Mach numbers over 
the test range in figures 4.4-1 through 4.4-4 for the original baseline descent 
configuration. · 
</p>
<p>In addition to verifying the basic aerodynamic characteristics of the SERV 
baseline configuration, specific vehicle geometry details were also studied 
in these tests. These were the effects on vehicle trim conditions and lift-
to-drag ratio caused by: · 
</p>
<p>1) removal of the engine doors

<p>.,· 
</p>
<p>2) .decrease in heat shield corner radius 
</p>
<p>3) increase in afterbody length 
</p>
<p>4) different upper and lower heat shield face corner raoii· 
</p>
<p>References 10 and 11 give complete graphic presentat'.ions .and digitJzed listings 
of the basic data .from these tests with comparisons of the geometric variables 
noted above. Figures 4.4-5 and 4.4-6 illustrate the general effect of heat 
shield corner radii -and re.moval of the aero spike engine protection doors. 
</p>
<p>For comparison purposes, figure 4.4-5 defines the effect-of heat shield corner 
radius at the trim conditions for a vehicle with a typical longitudinal CG. 
located at 0.2459 body diameters from the center of. the:heat shield face. 
Variation of trim angle of attack and resultant lift-to-dr:ag ratio ·with. lateral· 
CG offset are given for the various corner radius configurations. 
</p>
<p>This representative. Mach. number condition indicates .that dec1reasing the heat. 
shield corner radius from r/D = 0.11 to r/D == 0.05 does not significantly 
change the variation in tr.im angle of a.t:tack. However, d1.1e to the· higher lift­
to-drag characteristics at any angle of attack for the smaller corner.radius 
vehicle, it does increase the trim L/D,, Removal of the engine- doors not only 
reduces the trim angle of attack, ·but also produces ari even greater decrease 
in the _trim L/D. Finally, leaving the upper heat. shield c0rner radius at 
(r/D)u = 0.11 and reducing the lower radius to· (r/D)L = 0.05, creates an assy­
metric vehicle which will induce a higher trim angle of attack and resultant 
lift-to-drag ratio. 
</p>
<p>To further show the loss fa trim angle of attack and L/D by removal of the 
aerospike engine thermal protection doors, figure 4.4-6 presents the high 
supersonic test data extrapolated to the hypersonic Mach range by application 
of appropriate Apollo reference data and hypersonic ~low theory (the validity 
of this procedure is illustrated in more detail in section 2.2 of volume 4J 
Trim angle of attack and L/D are shown as a function of lateral and longitudi­
nal CG position. From these trends, it is apparent that even if engine doors 
could be eliminated as thermal protection, the loss in trim lift-to-drag would 
be unacceptable. An approximate 1.5-foot additional CG offset would be required 
to trim this vehicle at the same L/D as the baseline vehicle. 
</p>
<p>Schlieren photographs of bow shock shapes and flow patterns for the four con­
figurations discussed above are presented in figure 4.4-7. These flow field 
photographs are all at M=4.65 for an angle of attack very near the baseline 
vehicle nominally required trim angle of attack of a= 22°. 
</p>
<p>4.5 CONCLUSIONS 
</p>
<p>From the SERV descent scale model wind tunnel force tests, the following 
conclusions can be made. 
</p>
<p>The SERV baseline descent configuration can be trimmed at a reentry L/D of 
0 .3 with a CG offset of between 3\ to !+ feet. 
</p>

<p>The SERV baseline descent configuration is 1 adequately stable ina trimilled 
attitude throughout the reentry and terminal deceleration flight range.· 
</p>
<p>Removal of the aerospike engine .protection doors results in too large a de-
crease in trim lift-to-drag ratio to be feasible. · · 
</p>
<p>Reduction in heat shield corner raqius tends to increase trim lift-to•dr.ag
1 
</p>
<p>slightly. 1 
</p>
<p>Using a larger corner radi.us on the top side of the heat shield .and a signif-. 
icantly smaller radius on .the. lower side i.nduces. ae"i:odynamic moments which 
assist in trimming.·the. vehicle• •a,t higher. L/D for a given CG offset.

<p>SCOPE-
</p>
<p>·A feasibility study of a single-stage to orbit reusable vehicle (SERV) 
</p>
<p>is being conducted by the Chrysler Corporation. Integrated into the 
</p>
<p>base of this vehicle is an aerospik~-nozzle engin·e having a diameter 
</p>
<p>of approximately 89 feet. This large diameter allows for a very high 
</p>
<p>no.zzle expa11$ion are.a ratio and t~herefore high vacuum specific impulse. 
</p>
<p>In addition, the inherent altitude comperuiation capability of the 
</p>
<p>aerospike-nozzle provides high s~cific impulse throughout the vehicle 
</p>
<p>trajectory, from sea level to vacuum. 
</p>
<p>Under a 9 month contract, engine design, performance and operational 
</p>
<p>data were furnished byRocketdyne for_the feasibility evaluation of 
</p>
<p>the SERV concept. Performance data-have been generated parametrically 
</p>
<p>·. with vehicle and engine geometry. A baseline engine geometry was 
</p>
<p>selected on which wind tunnel studies were conducted, The objectives 
</p>
<p>of these tests were to provide data for predicting the ~ffect of the 
</p>
<p>external flow on full size engine performance over the SERV vehicle 
</p>
<p>range of operating conditions, This report presents the results of 
</p>
<p>· -· the model tests, the approach followed for evaluation and scaling of 
</p>
<p>test results to obtain full size SERV engine install_ed performance, 
</p>
<p>and the full size installed engine performance generated over the 
</p>
<p>range of flight Mach numbers and altitudes of interest for the 
</p>
<p>SERV Vehicle. 
</p>
<p>-.-~----
</p>
<p>A-2 
</p>
<p>!

<p>INTRODUCTION 
</p>
<p>Aerosp~ke engine specific impulse performance .is _obtainecl frotn the 
</p>
<p>evaluation of: a) .combustion and ,expan·sion in th,_e combµstor; b) expansion 
</p>
<p>in the nozzle and nozzle base, Fig. l; c)' combustion and expansion in the 
</p>
<p>gas.:..generator turbine systemsn, anti. d) frictiq~ ii:t the prope1lat1t flow 
</p>
<p>circuits. • These processes .. ,a1•e, eva.lua.tecl in. ter,ins· ,:o.~ :.e.ffJ.c,ienc,iei which, 
•!" • ., • . • 
</p>
<p>characterize the dej5l'ee to which id_eali ty is approached -in each process. 
</p>
<p>Weighed, summed, and multiplied by ?-deal specific :iinpuJse values, _these 
</p>
<p>efficiencies yield the predicted aerospil{e engine -s.pegt.fic .impulse. 
</p>
<p>' ' 
</p>
<p>. Extensive cold-flow and hot--firing tests have been .. conduct:eci to verify 
'- ·. .·' 
</p>
<p>the analytical and empirical methods and equations used, __ in, the. prediptip_n, 
</p>
<p>of these efficiencies for conventional aerospike geometries. Because of 
</p>
<p>_its high area ratio and short nozzle length the SERV is not considered a 
</p>
<p>conventional geometry. However, -except for the expapsion process 
</p>
<p>occurring at the bas-.e . .of the I?-ozz:l:e, all efficiencies in the SERV engine 
</p>
<p>are acc~rately determined using the methods previously developed for 
</p>
<p>conventional aerospikes. 
</p>
<p>The efficiency of the expansion at the base of the aerospike is character­
</p>
<p>ized by the base pressure, and is incorporated into the nozzle overall 
</p>
<p>thrust coefficient efficiency, CT. This efficiency evaluates the expansion 
</p>
<p>in the aerospike primary flowfield and in the nozzle base. Initial 
</p>
<p>estimates were made of this efficiency for use in the generation of. SERV 
</p>
<p>engine parametric data. A cold,.-flow program was conducted to improve 
</p>
<p>the reliability of the methods used to obtain CT estimates in still-air 
</p>
<p>and in slipstream operation,

</p>
<p>The cold-flow test program was-run using a scaled model (Fig. 2) of the 
</p>
<p>SERV vehicle 1 incorporating a high expansion area ratio, short length 
</p>
<p>aerospike engine model. These· tests were conducted in still-.air and in 
</p>
<p>slipstream1 at Rocketdyne's Rocket Nozzle Test Facility (RNTF, and at 
</p>
<p>Arnold Engineering Development Center (AEDC). 
</p>
<p>The slipstream wind tunnel program simulated low altitude external flow 
</p>
<p>conditions simi'1ar tcr those encountered by the vehicle in flight. These 
</p>
<p>tests were not-run at the pressure ratios of an actual trajectory. The 
</p>
<p>data were extra-polated to inct;t·cate the ~ffect of the slipstream on high 
</p>
<p>area rat;i.o· aerospike eng:j.ne opex•at;i.on·. Previous testing with aerospi-"ke · 
</p>
<p>nozzles of low area ratio_has shown that the· integrated effect of slip­
</p>
<p>stream on the complete trajectory is&lt; small. Slipstream testing at AEDC' 
</p>
<p>has provided information on.this aspect for the SERV engine geometry 
</p>
<p>and furnished· data used in scaling the slipstream effects .• · 
</p>
<p>Still-air testing of the baseline SERV engine geomery is a significant 
</p>
<p>. step in the test program since no aerospike of that area ratio (465-) and 
</p>
<p>nozzle percent length (5 percent) has been tested previously under cold-
</p>
<p>·. flow or hot-firing conditions. Testing under the SERV program provided 
</p>
<p>a: basis for the prediction of the basline engine performance and for 
</p>
<p>the parametric engine data. The body of SERV parametric data encompasses 
</p>
<p>a wide range of geometric parameters .as indicated in Table 1. The slip­
</p>
<p>stream testing constitutes the basis for correction of these data to 
</p>
<p>slipstream conditions. These tests pr.ovide data on the effect of engine 
</p>
<p>operation on vehicle base pressure and therefore vehicle base drag with 
</p>
<p>engine on • 
</p>
<p>·Engine performanoe estimates haye been revised based upon evaluation of 
</p>
<p>test results at RNTF and AEDC facilities. The cold flow program does not 
</p>
<p>provide absolute confirmation of the engine performance 1 but constitutes 
</p>
<p>the most economic way of determining if the SERV configuration can develop 
</p>
<p>the necessary open-wake base pressure to make concept feasible. 
</p>

<p>AEROSPIKE PERFORMANCE CORRELATIONS 
</p>
<p>A large body of data has been generated for the aerospike nozzle under 
</p>
<p>NASA, Air Force, and Rocketdyne sponsorship (Table 2). These data have 
</p>
<p>been used to develop a method of calculation· of overall aerospike nozzle 
</p>
<p>performance, This method predicts the primary nozzle contribution and 
</p>
<p>the secondary (base pressure). contribution to overall nozzle performance, 
</p>
<p>The primary contribution is. obtained·,:by using the method of character­
</p>
<p>istics, a iongtime proven method, The bulk of the analytical effort has 
</p>
<p>been placed in the development of base pressure calculation methods, 
</p>
<p>Successful theoretical and empirical,methods of base pressure predtction 
</p>
<p>were developed for operation at and near design pressure ratio. Past. 
</p>
<p>correlations of predicted values and experimental data have shown good 
</p>
<p>results in that pressure ratio range (Fig, 3), As may be noticed from 
</p>
<p>Fig, 3 1 the base pressure is invariant with pressure ratio in a range 
</p>
<p>of pressure ratios including design pressure ratio, _In this range, 
</p>
<p>known as the closed-wake region, ambient pressure is not a variable 
</p>
<p>affecting base pressure, The problem of prediction of base.pressure 
</p>
<p>here is therefore greatly simplified, In the range of pressure ratios 
</p>
<p>which includes sea level, the base pressure is affected by the ambient 
</p>
<p>pressure and its theoretical and empirical prediction is more complex, 
</p>
<p>Because of this, overall nozzle performance prediction is made in two 
</p>
<p>distinct ways, one for the closed-wake and one for the open-wake. 
</p>
<p>CLOSED WAKE OVERALL NOZZLE PERFORMANCE PREDICTION 
</p>
<p>In the closed-wake the performance of the aerospike engine is obtained 
</p>
<p>from the method of characteristics analysis of the primary and a calcu­
</p>
<p>lation of the base pressure, From these two analyses the primary and 
</p>
<p>secondary thrusts are computed, The methods used in the calculation of 
</p>
<p>these two· contributions are discussed below. 
</p>

<p>Primary Nozzle Thrust·Coeffici~nt, CF 
p 
</p>
<p>:_.,· 
</p>
<p>.This parameter is obtained for any altitude of op_eration from computerized 
</p>
<p>method of characteristics and boundary layer eq:.rntions. The methods are 
</p>
<p>very accurate and yield (for a given geometry) nozzle thrust coeffi.cie nt 
</p>
<p>and nozzle wall pressure values as.a function of nozzle length and .pressure 
</p>
<p>ratio. This parameter reflects the nozzle expansion efficiency &lt;1 ), the 
. g 
</p>
<p>effect of reaction kinetics &lt;7l_ k); the boundary layer drag ('1'/_d), and other 
</p>
<p>procese.es such as expansion around baffles &lt;1{_ b). The various efficiencies 
</p>
<p>combined with the ideal thrust coefficient yield the primary thrust co­
</p>
<p>efficient as shownin the following equation, 
</p>
<p>CF 
p 
</p>
<p>a[l+f(1{-l)i] CF 
ideal 
vac 
</p>
<p>Typical graphs resulting from this type of analysis are shown in Figs. 4 
</p>
<p>and 5. As can be seen in Fig. 4, the thrust coefficient decreases as 
</p>
<p>pressure ratio (altitude) increases. At high pressure ratios the thrust 
</p>
<p>coefficient becomes constant. This indicates that the nozzle wall pressures 
</p>
<p>are no longer affected by the prevailing ambient pressure, The primary 
</p>
<p>nozzle thrust coefficient presented in Fig. 4 follows the same trends with 
</p>
<p>pressure ratio as the primary intrinsic thrust of the nozzle. The intrin­
</p>
<p>sic thrust of the nozzle. The intrinsic thrust represents the force 
</p>
<p>developed by the engine exclusive of ambient pressure drag. 
</p>
<p>Closed-Wake Nozzle Base Pressure 
</p>
<p>The bas~ pressure of aerospike nozzles follows curves with pressure ratio 
</p>
<p>which are similar in behavior to that of the i ntrinsiG nozzle thrust 
</p>
<p>coefficient, Fig. 6, When altitude (pressure ratio) increases the base 
</p>
<p>pressure decreases, as ambient pressure waves transmitted through the 
</p>

<p>primary flowfield affect the base region, An altitude is eventually 
</p>
<p>reached where the ambient pres.~ure does not affect the base. The base 
</p>
<p>pressur~ remains constant from this point on and the wake is said to be 
</p>
<p>closed, 
</p>
<p>Two methods are used for prediction of base pressures in the closed-wake 
</p>
<p>regime, a theoretical method, and an empirical method.. The empirical 
</p>
<p>method generates only the bas-e pressure. · The theoretical method, 
</p>
<p>Append:!.x A, is more encompassing and ·generates more information about 
</p>
<p>the flowfield in the base, The results and accuracy of. the empirical 
</p>
<p>and theoretical·methods are comparable, 
</p>
<p>. For the SERV engine several of the parameters of Table 1 are beyond the 
</p>
<p>range of aerospike test experience. However, the equations were assumed 
</p>
<p>applicable following similar experiences in aerospike programs wherein 
</p>
<p>equations based on the lower expansion area ratio were successfully 
</p>
<p>extended to an area ratio of 150.

p

</p>
<p>characteristics analysis (Fig. 4). Empirical methods as well as an 
</p>
<p>extension of the closed-wake theoretical method .(Appendix B) have been

</p>
<p>ratio are normalized to a considerable degree (Fig. SL This ts

<p>Thls technique has been used and found to have less uncertainty than the

<p>There.are two ways in which this method is used: 1. Where hot-firing 
</p>
<p>performance is desired for a gometry which ha~ been ccild-flow teStE!d, the 
</p>
<p>normalized CT plot is obtained from the cold-flow data for the :exact 
</p>
<p>geometry and usedfor hot-firing calculations. This approach has the 
</p>
<p>least uncertainty and has been verified in previous tests. 2. Where 
</p>
<p>parametric performance is desired and.only a few geometries have been 
</p>
<p>tested, a_ universal type of normalized CT is used, This type of plot 
</p>
<p>uses all the data generated to date plotted in the same manner as shown 
</p>
<p>in Fig. 8. This universal plot has a higher uncertainty associated with 
</p>
<p>it, but is suitable for parametric evaluations,

</p>
<p>parametric engine data have been generated using the.latter method for 
</p>
<p>the «:&gt;pen-wake, The baseline engine performance will be updated when the 
</p>
<p>cold-::tlow model is tested and its "normalized CT" is obtained.

<p>RELATIVE IMPORTANCE OF SERV ENGINE BASE PRESSURE 
</p>
<p>A comparison of the base pressure effects on nozzle performance in the 
</p>
<p>SERV aerospike negine and in a conventional engine is depicted in Fig, 9, 
1 
</p>
<p>Here the thrust coefficient efficiency ?{ C has been used, 'l'his efficiency 
. F 
</p>
<p>is equal to the nozzle thrust coefficient divided by the ideal vacuum thrust 
</p>
<p>coefficient, The gain in performance with small amounts of secondary flow 
</p>
<p>is again greater in·the SERV engine than in the conventional aerospike. 
</p>
<p>At sea level the base pressure. contribution becomes more significant in. 
</p>
<p>the SERV engine. Its magnitude is comparable to that of the primary 
</p>
<p>contribution. 
</p>
<p>IMP.ACT OF SLIPSTREAM 
</p>
<p>' __ , 
When operating in the slipstream, and during a relatively short interval 
</p>
<p>of time, the vehicle provides an atmosphere unfavorable for efficient 
</p>
<p>expans.ion of the aerospike engine gases, at the altitude of operation. 
</p>
<p>As a result,_ the nozzle thrust coefficient (and therefore thrust and 
</p>
<p>specific impulse) is less than would ~e expected for that altitude, To 
</p>
<p>get the proper appraisal of the situation one must look at the effect as 
</p>
<p>it relates to time. 
</p>
<p>On a vehicle in flight there are two types of forces in action: a retarding 
</p>
<p>force (drag) caused by the slipstream and vehicle body form (or design), 
</p>
<p>and an accelerating force provided by the engine, The retarding fo;rce 
</p>
<p>changes with the vehicle velocity and is practically independent of the 
</p>
<p>type of_engine installed in ·the vehicle base. The acceleratiµg force is 
</p>
<p>· the engine intrinsic thrust, characterized by the intrinsic thrust coef­
</p>
<p>ficient (Fig, 7). In the absence of adverse slipstream effects, this 
</p>
<p>coefficient would decrease with time along the trajectory as shown in Fig. 10, 
</p>
<p>a replot of Fig,7 with time as abcissa, The time scale was obtained from a 
</p>
<p>SERV Vehicle trajectory, 
</p>
<p>A-21 
</p>

<p>Slipstream Effect on Aerospike ·Thrust Coefficient~T 
INT 
</p>
<p>In th(;) presence of slipstream, the engine, installed in the wake of the 
</p>
<p>vehicle, sees an effective ambient pr.essurE;l. This causes the flow to 
</p>
<p>effectively a.djust ~o a higher altitude so that the intrinsic thrust 
</p>
<p>produced is t_hat Which would be experienced at a higher dtitude. This 
</p>
<p>means 'that the intrinsic thrust coefficient would be displaced in time 
</p>
<p>as shown in. F:i.g. 10, an estim.ate of. the effect of slipgtream on the 
</p>
<p>SERV engine. 
</p>
<p>In previous slipstream test-programs (Table 2) it was observed.that the 
</p>
<p>effect of the pseudo-altitude is the only effect on nozzle performance. 
</p>
<p>·That is, expansion of the gases. proceQ.."is .as if only the altitude had been 
</p>
<p>change, and. no extraneous recompress.ions occur ;Ln the nozzle. or nozzl.e 
</p>
<p>base. This was confirmed in two previously conducted slipstream programs. 
' ·The original data of both programs is shown in Figs. lla and 12a in terms 
</p>
<p>of nozzle CT coefficient and base pressure ratios plotted against the 
</p>
<p>.altitude pressure ratio P /P.o,,o. When the data is recomputed in terms of-c / 
the vehicle base pressure ratio and plotted versus Pc/PBVEH' the results 
</p>
<p>indicated in Figs. llb and 12b were obtained, This shows altitude effects 
</p>
<p>alone have acted upon CT and_the base pressure, so that the vehicle base 
</p>
<p>pressure PBVEH is a correlating parameter for both nozzle efficien_cy CT 
</p>
<p>and base pressure ratio (Figs., llb and 12b). The vehicle base pressure, 
</p>
<p>PBVEH could not, however, be used as a correlating parameter for the SERV 
</p>
<p>engine, The base pressure of the SERV in slipstream corresponded to a 
</p>
<p>still air value at an ambient pressure somewhere between PBVEH and P00 • 
</p>
<p>These .results are discussed in the Slipstream Model Test Results section.

<p>SERV CQ~.FLOW TEST MOD~L 
</p>
<p>The SERV cold-flow ,test model (Fig. l) has a diameter of 27 inches at the 
</p>
<p>base and a. length of 20 inches. It has a double cone forebody and a semi- I, 
</p>
<p>spherical reentry type heat shield base. The model is attached to a cylin­
</p>
<p>drical forebody of approximately 126 inches in length. A strut attached 
</p>
<p>to the cylindrical forebody supported the model during tests at AEDC. 
</p>
<p>Tli.e SERV vehicle model incorpora.tes a scaled model of the SERV aerospike 
</p>
<p>engine. The engine model has been designed for air operation at a chamber 
</p>
<p>pressure of 400 psia. With a mass· flow approaching 11 pounds p·er second, 
</p>
<p>the model develops approximately 730 lb of thrust with a thrust coeffi­
</p>
<p>cient of approximately 1.6. Placed at a diameter of approximately. 26 inches 
</p>
<p>the throat annulus has a width of approximately 0.014 inches, This latter 
</p>
<p>dimens.ion was an important factor in the selection of the model size. The 
</p>
<p>more critical model geornetrid parameters are summarized in Table 3. 
</p>
<p>·MODEL SCALING 
</p>
<p>Complete simulation of aerospike engine CT performance and base pressures 
</p>
<p>is achieved when geometry I specific heat ratio ( r ) I and boundar:,r layer 
</p>
<p>conditions are matched between engine a:nd model. Matching of geometry and 
</p>
<p>specific heat ratio ensures identical dimensionless primary inviscid flow­
</p>
<p>field prop.erties (exit Mach num.ber, design pressure ratio, and exit flow 
</p>
<p>angles). Equal boundary layer conditions ensure equal dimensionless primary 
</p>
<p>nozzle friction loss and base pres13ure ratios. When all three conditions 
</p>
<p>are satisfied nozzle thrust coefficient efficiencies (CT) of model and 
</p>
<p>engine are identical (except for kinetic and gas relaxation effects). 
</p>
<p>Numerous aerospike test programs have indicated that nozzle geometry (char­
</p>
<p>acterized by nozzle area ratio, percent length, and nozzle contour) is the 
</p>
<p>key parameter in the correlation of nozzle CT performance. Relying on 
</p>
<p>this experimental evidence, the SERV engine was scaled geometrically from 
</p>

<p>MODEL NOZZLE CONTOUR DESIGN 
</p>
<p>A truncated isentropicspike contour is the. basic geometry from 
</p>
<p>which the final SERV nozzle contour.is obtained. In this basic geowetry 
</p>
<p>the expansion of the gases centers about a point on the nozzle throat 
</p>
<p>resulting in external. expansion of the gases. For the SERV configuration 
</p>
<p>a two-dimensional.doubli wall section replaces a section of the basic 
</p>
<p>isentropic spike c.ontour near the throat. The resulting geomet:V.y: expands 
</p>
<p>the gases internally in the dbuble wall section and externally in the 
</p>
<p>truncated isentropic spike section, Careful matching of the two sections 
</p>
<p>is made to retain the same .external flowfield as the basic truncated 
</p>
<p>isentropic nozzle contour. This insures equal nozzle performance of the 
</p>
<p>SE~V.and the truncated isentropic spike geometries. 
</p>
<p>The model nozzle contour design resulting from geometry scaling alone is 
</p>
<p>presented in Fig. 13. Since the specific heat ratio of the model test 
</p>
<p>medium is 1.4 and that of the SERV engine combustion gases have an effective 
</p>
<p>value of 1.16, the nozzle contours differ slightly. 
</p>

</p>
<p>Rocketdyne. The following discussion on the overall SERV model design and 
</p>
<p>drawings are taken from a Chrysler document (Ref. 2, revised where applic­

<p>The test .model is a 2.5 percent scale, stainless steel repli·ca of the aft 
</p>
<p>portion of the SERV Launch Vehicle. It has a di~met.er of 27 inches at 
</p>
<p>the base and is approximately 20 inches in length.. The model has a ddubl:e 
</p>
<p>cone forebody consisting of 45 deg and 25 deg half· angle conical frusti·unis. 
</p>
<p>·and a s·emi-spherical reentry type heat shield 'base. The model will be 
</p>
<p>. mounted on a strut supported cylincr'ical ft&gt;rebody that i's 6. 68 _inches in 
</p>
<p>diameter and 126 inches in length. A drawing of the model geometry is 
</p>
<p>shown .in Fig. 13. 
</p>
<p>The model has a scaled 5 percent length aerospike engine incorporat.ed in 
</p>
<p>the base and is designed to determine axial force, engine performance and 
</p>
<p>bo~y static pressure di~tribution duri~g cold~fl6w j~t simulation_testing. 
</p>
<p>The· model engine combustion chamber, nozzle, and nozzle contour have be.en 
</p>
<p>designed for air operation, at a chamber pressure of 400 psia and at mass 
</p>
<p>flows of 11 pounds per second. Internal ducting is provided in the model 
</p>
<p>to bleed off .a,pproximat.ely 2.1 percent of the engine mass flow from the 
</p>
<p>air manifold to the turbine exhaust annulus located in the base just 
</p>
<p>inboard of the aerospike ramp. 
</p>
<p>The air manifold which feeds the aerospike engine is the main model support 
</p>
<p>structure. This manifold consists of a center barrel, three rows ~f radial 
</p>
<p>tubes, a ring flange, and a set of fl·exures at the forward end. The center 
</p>
<p>barrel mates with the model sting at a socket joint and is secured with a 
</p>
<p>Marman clamp. Two rows of 10 thin-walled radial tubes are brazed to the 
</p>
<p>center barrel and to the outer ring flange. These tubes duct air from the 
</p>
<p>center barrel to the engine and provide radial support for the model. A 
</p>
<p>third row of 10 radial tubes are brazed to the cente·r barrel and to the aft 
</p>
<p>section of the ring flange to duct air to the turbine exhaust annulus. An 
</p>
<p>orifice place is provided in the center barrel to meter the turbine exhaust 
</p>
<p>mass flow. 
</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>An axial ring flexure is secured to the :foreward end of the center barrel 
</p>
<p>and provides radial support and roll restraint :for the model.. lt&gt;d.el axial 
</p>
<p>restraint is provided by a 1000-pound lqad cell mounted between universal 
</p>
<p>flexures and secured between the center. barrel and the model thrust 
</p>
<p>structure. 
</p>
<p>The aerospike ramp, including the inboard combustion chamber, inboard nozzle· 
</p>
<p>surface·, and turbine exhaust mani.fold is secured to the ring flange with 
</p>
<p>socket heat cap screws. The outboard combustion chamber and exit nozzle 
</p>
<p>surface mates with the ramp section in a slip joint. and is secured with 
</p>
<p>· socket heat cap screws which will allow exact spacing of the nozzle throat 
</p>
<p>gap. O--ring seals are provided at all joints. 
</p>
<p>Tlle model base plate is attached to the aerospike ramp at a flange type 
</p>
<p>Joint. ·The base plate provides access to the model for final assembly and 
</p>
<p>connection of model instrumentation. The plate also has provisions for an 
. . 
</p>
<p>alternate turbine exhaust dump location • 
</p>
<p>.One set of engine reentry protection doors is provided with the model. 
</p>
<p>These do.ors are fabricated as split rings for ease of handling and installa­
</p>
<p>tion on the model. The doors are attached to the outboard nozzle rlng with 
</p>
<p>socket head cap screws. 
</p>
<p>The first forebody section consists of the 25 deg frustrum, and is attached 
</p>
<p>to the air manifold ring flange at the aft end and to the ring flexure at 
</p>
<p>the foreward end at flange joints. This section also includes the model 
</p>
<p>thrust structure and transmits model axial forces to the load cell. 
</p>
<p>The second model forebody section consists of the 45 deg frustrum and is 
) 
</p>
<p>attached to the first forebody section at a flange joint. This section 
</p>
<p>provides access for connection of model instrumentation. 
</p>

</p>

<p>structural support and houses four inlet ·air tubes. The leading and 
</p>
<p>. trailing edges of the strut are removal&gt;le for. routing instrumentati.(?11$. 
</p>
<p>Spacers.will be provided for placement between the strut and the tunnel 
</p>
<p>floor so that+ 10 deg angle of attack may be achieved.

<p>ENGINE PERFQRMANQE REVIEW 
</p>
<p>BASELINE ENGINE PERFORMANCE 
</p>
<p>· A complete description of the SERV baseline engine design, engine balance, 
</p>
<p>and compc;ment. efficiency parameters is show11 in Table 4 • The data in the 
</p>
<p>tab~e traces the development of the i11itial baseline engine to its present 
</p>
<p>geometry •. In th~s table values are furnished for the various process 
</p>
<p>efficiencies _whose combfned effect. determine the engine specific impulse. 
</p>
<p>System geometry and flowrates as well as temperature and pressure schedules 
</p>
<p>ar·e ·µresented in the tab.le. The performance shown is the nominal engine 
</p>
<p>p_erformance. based on :the nominal efficiencies shown in the table. 
</p>
<p>ENGINE REBALANCE 
</p>
<p>Since the initial baseline engine performance estimate was prepared (Ref. 3 ) 1 
</p>
<p>studies have been conducted to provide a 10:1 throttling capability in the 
</p>
<p>SERV engine, to evaluate the turbomachinery efficiency values, to refine 
</p>
<p>the nozzle contour and the respective nozzle divergency and drag effici­
</p>
<p>encies, and the fuel heat ga:i.n in the jacket cooling process. As a result 
</p>
<p>of these refinem·ents, an increase in baseline performance can be predicted. 
</p>
<p>A redu_ction in turbine flow requirements for the engine was also indicated. 
</p>
<p>The reduction in secondary flow results in part of the increased engine I. 
s 
</p>
<p>These studies indicated that turbine flow requirements coµld be reduced to 
</p>
<p>0.0264. To provide throttling of the engine to 10 percent of its nominal 
</p>
<p>thrust., the oxidizer injector pressure drop had to be increased to 2000 
</p>
<p>psia to insure proper combustion in the chamber. 
</p>
<p>The increased pressure drop required an oxidizer pump discharge of 4320_ 
</p>
<p>psia. This additional requirement on the turbomachinery was met by increas­
</p>
<p>ing the turbine flow ratio ( b" ) to 0,0295. 
</p>

<p>10:1 Throttling 
Capability 
</p>
<p>Structural studies have been conducted recently which resulted in the incor­
</p>
<p>poration of the engine thrust ring with existing vehicle structural elements. 
</p>
<p>This action resulted in a weight savings, It also allowed for the nozzle 
</p>
<p>throat to be placed at a larger diameter and at a position closer to the 
</p>
<p>propellant tanks. Two changes in engine geometry resulted from the above 
</p>
<p>alterations: the nozzle expansion area ratio increased as well as the 
</p>
<p>nozzle length. Both of these changes have led to improved engine perfor­
</p>
<p>mance as shown in Table 6,

<p>NOZZLE PRIMARY TJIBUST COEFFICIENT

</p>
<p>characteristics analysis of the primary flowfield, It was calculated 
</p>
<p>for pressure ratios from simulated sea level (450) to. v::icutim •. Results. 
</p>
<p>are shown in Fig. 16. Re.compression of the nozz.le can be seen to end 
</p>
<p>at a pressure ratio of 440 as indicated by the constancy of .the primary 
</p>
<p>thrust coefficient.

<p>MEASURED MODEL PERFORMANCE 
</p>
<p>. When the slipstream model test. prq.gram was qi:iginally formu~ated, it was 
</p>
<p>recognized that the engine thrust would not be directly measured during 
</p>
<p>the slipstream rull!=l, but could. b~ ,isolated from the force balanc.e measure­
</p>
<p>ments of thrust-plus-vehicle-drag, using engine-off and engine-on runs at 
</p>
<p>identical slipstream conditions. The test procedure was designed to 
</p>
<p>provide pairs of runs consisting, o:f,an· engine.:.on case· followed by an .. 
</p>
<p>engine-off case at as nearly tbe same slipstream Mach number and total 
</p>
<p>pressure as was practically obtainable. · The difference in force balance 
</p>
<p>readings between each pair of runs would then be the engine thrust, plus 
</p>
<p>the force due to the change in vehicle drag due to the engine, plus error 
</p>
<p>forces due to changes in run conditions between the engine-on :cas.e and 
</p>
<p>corresponding engine-off case, as well as· any shift in the. force,. balance 
</p>
<p>zero due to pressure and temperature induced loads caused by the engine 
</p>
<p>gas supply flowing in the model. The model was designed to minimize the 
</p>
<p>zero shift, and this effect was assumed to be negligible in planning the 
</p>
<p>test program. In monitoring the on··line data during t.he testing, it was 
</p>
<p>found _that internal stresses i apparently produced by a temperature 
</p>
<p>difference between the model and.the nozzle supply air, caused force 
</p>
<p>balance loads that would be an important factor in interpreting the 
</p>
<p>test results. 
</p>
<p>From the results of a pair of runs, an engine-on case followed by an 
</p>
<p>engine~off case at the same slipstream conditions, the engine thrust 
</p>
<p>can be obtained, 
</p>
<p>The force balance load, FAC, opposes the net force on the model (Fig. 19). 
</p>

<p>Having determined that the originally selected method of.obtaining 
</p>
<p>performance from the test data could introduce considerable error, an 
</p>
<p>alternative method was used to check the sensitivity of the final results 
</p>
<p>to the method used, It was observed that for chamber pressure/cowl pressure 
</p>
<p>(P /PBV) of more than 400, the nozzle wall pressures were independent of 
C . 
</p>
<p>· slipstream. Ma.ch number and P c/P00 • Figure 20 compares_ experimental .and 
</p>
<p>theoretical wall pressures. The experimental pressures are: higher than 
</p>

</p>
<p>compared to t. = 465 used for the predictions. The theoretical results 
</p>
<p>indicated that the wall pressure should be constant for P /PBV &gt; 450. 
C 
</p>
<p>For P /PBV greater than 400, the intrinsic thrust of the no.zzle, excluding 
C . 
</p>
<p>the nozzle ·base, F , is therefore constant, 
pint

<p>If the value of F' were 
. Pint 
</p>
<p>knovt, the nozzle thrust at various slipstream condit.ions could be obtained 
</p>
<p>without using the force balance outputs, either for engine-on or engine-off,

<p>F = JS: + PBE x Ab - poO A 
e"'° Pint e 
</p>
<p>where 
</p>
<p>PBE is the integrated average base pressure 
</p>
<p>Pell is the freestream ambient static pressure 
</p>
<p>Ab is the base ar·ea 
</p>
<p>A is. the area of the vehicle base occupied by the engine 
e 
</p>
<p>In this case the accuracy of the results is independent of the force 
</p>
<p>readings for a given case, but is dependent on a pressure integration.

<p>. , EXTRAPOLATION OF PERFORMANCE TO HIGHER PRESSURE RATIOS 
</p>
<p>.. The SERV model expansion area ratio was 417 as tested. A one,...dimensional 
</p>
<p>isentropic expansion of air to an expansion ratio of 417 gives an e,cit 
-4 
</p>
<p>pressure/total pressure of ,336 x 10 ; The design pressure ratio, P~, 
</p>
<p>is the chamber pressure to ambient pressure ratio where the ex.it pressure 
' ' -4 
</p>
<p>equals ambient press:ure, which in this case is 1/.336 x 10 = 29800. 
</p>
<p>For the full scale 0i/Hi engine with an expansion ratio of 483, PRn=ll000. 
</p>
<p>Model performance can best be s.caled to hot firing performance by using 
</p>
<p>the normalized performance met"hod,. described in an earlier s~ction, This 
· PR PR 
</p>
<p>method states that if AA . = Pit , 
D Model . . . D Full 
</p>
<p>then CT = CT ., 
CT C 
</p>
<p>D Model TD Full 
</p>
<p>where CT is· the,, nozzle ef:Uciency--at the design pressure-- ratio. For&gt;,_a 
. D 
</p>
<p>typical SERV. trajectory,M
00 
</p>
<p>= 0, ,06, .8, .9, 1,1 and 1,25 occurred at_ 
</p>
<p>P /Po,,t&gt; = 135, 260, 390, 480, 740 and 1000 respectively. The equiv.alent 
C . ~ 
</p>
<p>model pressure ratios are Pc/Pa- Full x D Model, which are 365, 700, 
</p>
<p>· p~ Full 
.1050, 1300, 2000 and 2700. 
</p>
<p>The test conditions run for the model fall short of the required pressure 
</p>
<p>ratios for all flight Mach numbers except M- ~ o. The model data must 
</p>
<p>therefore be ·extrapolated to determine performance at ·the equivalent 
</p>
<p>trajectory pressur.e ratios. The extrapolation could be reduced to an 
</p>
<p>interpola.tion by anchoring a high pressure ratio point on each M00 curve. 
</p>
<p>The high pressure ~atio points used were the computed points at which 
</p>
<p>the performance departed from the theoretical uncompensated performance 
</p>
<p>curve for each slipstream Mach number. 
</p>

</p>

<p>INSTALLED PERFORMANCE 
</p>
<p>Figure 25 shows nozzle effi6ien_cies with the vehicle with doors on and 
</p>
<p>o-ff. The eff~ct of the doors on the nozzle is complex. For some pressure 
</p>
<p>ratio/Mach number cases, the doors ·cause a reduced PBV which reduces nozzle 
</p>
<p>base pressur.Et, ·and therefore reduc;:es nozzle efficiency.·· For .ot'her conditions, 
' ' 
</p>
<p>·the no~zle jet impinges on the doors, constraining the nozzle expansion 
</p>
<p>th~reby increasing base pressure. Referring to Fig. 24, at Mw = 9 and 
</p>
<p>PR= 800~ PB~= ,36 with doors and ,30 without. In this case the doors 
</p>
<p>'increase PBE, and from Fig. 25 it cari be- seen that for these conditions 
</p>
<p>the doors-on CTOO.is 2 percent more than the doors,--off· performance, 
</p>
<p>Similarly, at M~ = l,25·a·nd PR = 800 1 PBE is .;LS with doors on and .22 
</p>
<p>·with doors off, in which case the doors-off CTa.o is 2 percent. higher .than 
</p>
<p>for doors-off.

<p>The large effect of the doors on vehicle 
</p>
<p>dr'ag at high pressure ratios is apparently due to .. impingement of the 
</p>
<p>Elxpanding engine jet on the inside of the doors. The high pressure 
</p>
<p>impingement region inside the doors has a relatively large projected 
</p>
<p>area normal to the engine centerline, and is opposed by a relatively 
</p>
<p>low pressure out-sid.e the doors over the same projected area. Figure 27 
</p>
<p>shows the measured static pressures inside and outside the doors for the 
</p>
<p>condit:fons tested, Figure 28 compares installed.performance .for doors-on 
</p>
<p>and doors-off, At high pressure ratios installed performance, increases 
</p>
<p>of 5 to 10 percent were obtained by removing the doors. These performance 
</p>
<p>gains were relative to the tested doors position which was clearly a·poor 
</p>
<p>configuration, due to the exhaust impingement that occurred at high pressure. 
</p>
<p>ratios for this doors position. Performance approaching the doors-off 
</p>
<p>v'alues should be obtained if the doors are swung out to avoid impingement 
</p>
<p>and a vent is provided to. increase PBV relative to P
00
</p>
<p>• The doors-off 
</p>
<p>performance is representative of the performance expected from a configu­
</p>
<p>ration resulting from a doors optimization program. 
</p>
<p>The doors-qff model ACT • values shown in Fig. 26 are strongly dependent 
D 
</p>
<p>(approximately 1 to 1) on the.vehicle cowl area, Ad, which is proportionally 
</p>
<p>much greater than the corresponding area on the full scale vehicle, The 
</p>
<p>over-size cowl area was required for the model to maintain the throat 
</p>
<p>deflection within allowable limits. For an optimized full scale vehicle 
</p>
<p>ACT would approach zero, therefore in this way doors-off CT values are 
D I 
</p>
<p>conservative,

<p>APPENDIX A 
</p>
<p>BASE·PRESSUilE THEORY 
</p>
<p>Uocketdyne became interested in the base flow problem becau.se of the 
</p>
<p>neccssi ty of calculatb1g the base pressure to complete the thrust cal cul a-• 
</p>
<p>tions, for the aerospike nozzJe.. In this nozzle~ high-pressure gos is ex­
</p>
<p>hausted from an annular-type. combus.tion chamber with low-'pressure turbin·e 
</p>
<p>exhaust gas introduced through a base. region (Fig. A-1 ) • Part of the 
</p>
<p>thrust .contribution normally attdbuted to a full-length nozzle is obtained 
</p>
<p>by the pressure of the se.condary gases acting over the base region and, 
</p>
<p>therefore, calculation of base pressure is an important part of overall 
</p>
<p>performance. evaluation. Becaµse the. centerbody is truncate.&amp;; .. the ,p:rimary 
. . . ' 
</p>
<p>flow separates from the end of the nozzle and e·ncloses a near:--wake region 
</p>
<p>aft of the base. The turbine. e•xhaust .gas bled into the· base region tends 
</p>
<p>to keep the recirculating hot gases off of the base surface. In addition, 
</p>
<p>the introduction of base bleed gives rise to higher.base pressures. 
</p>
<p>For the purpose of analysis, the base pressure problem was convenilintly 
</p>
<p>divided into two regimes: zero_. and small-bleed, and large-bleed. In 
</p>
<p>the former regime, the near wake is characterized by a recirculating flow 
</p>
<p>pattern, whereas, i.n the latter regime, the bleed rate is sufficient to 
</p>
<p>suppress the reverse-flow component. It has been found that the inner 
</p>
<p>free jet boundary has the general shape shown in Fig •. A-1. This shape 
</p>
<p>differs from the usual free jet boundary shape for flow over cylinders 
</p>
<p>because the expansion waves from the flow expanding at the outer lip in­
</p>
<p>tersect the inner jet boundary and cause it to be concave upward. Because 
</p>
<p>of this inner jet boundary shape, it was found that; for the }'large:..bleed" 
</p>
<p>re~ime, the flow through the central core was much like the flow through 
</p>
<p>a converging-diverging nozzle with choking occurring at minimum ar~a. In 
</p>
<p>the case of flow over a cylind~r, this type of flow configuration will 
</p>
<p>not exist because the constant pressure free jet boundary tends to curve 
</p>
<p>into the axis of symmetry, so that a minimum area cannot occur. Therefore, 
</p>
<p>· only the small-blee9- theory will be applicable for flow over cylinders. 
</p>
<p>-,·--~--~ .. 
</p>
<p>A-81
