<p>------•--•--~•-----••••••-• &lt;v~.,_._, __ ,_., ...., .... ..,_,-,,__•L•;.,..,l,U~.~,,:-~~~i..Ll• .. ,'1.,,3•.;c~hl.1-"~li.•!,2'..;J4! . .').".:.i:~'J_,,-.&amp;,_~,.'U,L:~.-;t:,JMMJ';}"4':~~~ 
</p>
<p>It shriuld be mentioned that the terms small-bleed and large~bleed were 
</p>
<p>· introduced to def inc the flow configurations for the case· in ,,hich re­
</p>
<p>circulation is present and the case in which recirculation clocs not 
</p>
<p>occur. Therefore, the small-bleed theory might actually be used on flow 
</p>
<p>fields in which substantial amounts of base bleed are introduced. The 
</p>
<p>details of this theory are presented below. 
</p>
<p>KOHST TWO-DIMENSIONAL· MODEL . 
</p>
<p>For the zero- and small-bleed case, the Korst two,,-dimensional model 
</p>
<p>(Hef. A-1} has been taken as a starting point. in its original form, 
</p>
<p>· this model considers a supersonic primary .flow field expanding ise.ntrop­
</p>
<p>ically into a lower pressure base-region (Fig. A-2). The. thickness of 
</p>
<p>the boundary layer before separation is assumed to be negligible. Most 
</p>
<p>of this flow field is inviscid, but there exists a turbulent slie·ar layer 
</p>
<p>in ~hich mixing occurs between the primary flow and the recirculating fluid 
</p>
<p>near the base. The width of this mixi~g zone is proportional to its length. 
</p>
<p>Two •important stream! ines in the shear layer are defined by continuity. , 
</p>
<p>The jet bo~ndary streamline originates at the point of flow separation, //· · .•. 
</p>
<p>and divides primary flo.w from base flow, The discriminating streamline / ' : 
</p>
<p>separates the fluid which passes through the wake from that whicli redrcn- ,1 
</p>
<p>!ates back to the base. Any bleed introduced into the base region is · · / 
-i 
</p>
<p>With no bleed, the two are identicaJ. / bounded by these two streamlines. 
</p>
<p>. 
The 1;1tatic pressure is assumed to be constant from the initial expansion 
</p>
<p>until the recompression region is reached. At that point, the inviscid 
</p>
<p>flow, ~hich has been converging toward the axis of symriletry,, is turned 
</p>
<p>paralle~ tothe axis with an accompanying pressure rise. This pressure 
</p>
<p>g~adient is· imposed on the turbulent shear layer, causingdecelera~ion 
</p>
<p>and, for the slower elements, 
</p>
<p>The discriminating streamline 
</p>
<p>a reversal of flow back into the base region. 
</p>
<p>stagnates in the adverse pressure g,i{•adient. , 
' All higher velocity elements penetrate the recompression region hnd escape 
</p>
<p>into the far wake; all those po,ssessing -lower velocity are turned back. 
</p>

<p>APPLICATION TO AXISYMMETHIC GEOHETUY 
</p>
<p>J/orapplication of the Korst flow model to axisymmetric geometry, con­
</p>
<p>sideration must, be given to the distortion in.shape of the annular stream 
</p>
<p>tubes as they approach the axis. In the absence of longitudinal density 
</p>
<p>. and velocity ·gradients, the cross-sectional area of .an annular str~rim tube 
</p>
<p>·would be constant. Therefore·, as the mean radius of the shear layer de­
</p>
<p>creased, thewidth of the individual stream tubes would.increase, and 
</p>
<p>there would be an accompanying distortion of: the overall velocity profile. 
</p>
<p>'In Fig. .\.-4,, a hypothetical step-function profile is use_d to illustrate 
</p>
<p>the effect. At location A-A, each stream tube of the profile has the 
</p>
<p>same annular flow··axeac .• · At section ·B-B, the areas are unchanged, but it 
' . . . 
</p>
<p>can be seen that the total radial width of the profile, as well as the 
</p>
<p>relative widths or" the two stream tubes, ar~ quite different. 
</p>
<p>In the present method, it is assumed that the normal shear layer spreading 
</p>
<p>rate observed in two-dimensional flow and the radial distortion effect 
</p>
<p>are additive. That is, if axisymmetric effects were reduc~d by moving 
</p>
<p>the actual shear layer out to a position of large constant radius, the 
</p>
<p>velocity profile and geometric dimensions would approach those of a two­
</p>
<p>dimensional configuration. To quantify this concept, a hypothetical shear 
</p>
<p>layer is considered, having the same origin as the actual shear layer, 
</p>
<p>and characterized by the same spreading parameter {Fig. A-5). The jet 
</p>
<p>boll!ldary streamline, which originates at the separation.point, rernains at 
</p>
<p>a distance R from the axis. It is assumed that the thickness of the shea:i;-
. . 0 . 
</p>
<p>layer is sufficiently small compared to R so that the two-dimensional· 
. 0 . 
</p>
<p>error-function profile is applicable. Under the foregoing assu:mptions 1 
</p>
<p>· the mnss flow between any two streamlines in the hypothetical shear layer 
</p>
<p>is equal to the corresponding flow in the actual shear layer, In addition, 
</p>
<p>the radial position of any streamline in the actual shear layer, as a 
</p>
<p>function of :i.c, can' be determined from its position in the hypothetical 
</p>
<p>shear layer by use of the fact that stream-tube flow areas are equal. 
</p>

<p>RECOMPRESSION HODEL. 
</p>
<p>To close the. base pressure solution, the 'id·en:t.Lty of the discriminating 
</p>
<p>streamline must bedetermined by enetgy relationships in the recompression 
</p>
<p>region. In the two-dimensional model, Korst postulated that the energy 
</p>
<p>on the discriminating streamline, in te-rms of the ratio o'f stagnation 
</p>
<p>pressure to static pressure, is equal to the pressure rise associated 
</p>
<p>with the turning of the inviscid flow behind the near wake (Fig. A-2 ). 
</p>
<p>The axisymmetric case presents unique problems, however, becnuse .the 
</p>
<p>flow angle along the ·corresponding inviscid jet boundary is constantly 
</p>
<p>changing as a func-tion of X. Therefore, considern~le emphasis hns been 
</p>
<p>placed on defining the flow geometry in the nft portion of the near wake 
</p>
<p>to determine the value of X at which recompression effectively occurs • 
</p>
<p>As the inviscid flow, with the superimposed shear layer, approaches the 
</p>
<p>axis, a point will be reached at which the forward-moving velocity profile 
</p>
<p>intersects the reverse flow (Fig. A-9 ). Because of spatf~l considerations, 
</p>
<p>the inviscid flow must supsequently turn, causing an associated pressure 
</p>
<p>rise (rccompression). This adverse pressure gradient is imposed on the 
</p>
<p>viscous· flow, causing the less energetic elements to reverse direction 
</p>
<p>progressively nntl join the recirculating stream. Because there is only 
</p>
<p>a ~mal~ pressure rise associated with the flow reversal of the low­
</p>
<p>velocity "tail" of the error-function profile, recompression is not 
</p>

</p>
<p>"ciive" into the axis, the higher the N-factor· for a given Crocc,1 number ..

<p>T4e experimental data used to o~tain ~he N~factor correlations covered a 
</p>
<p>wide variety of flow conditions, i.e., gas compositions 1 free stream Mach 
</p>
<p>numbert in&gt;i tia-1 boundary. layer ,.moment thickri.ess, .. degree of nommiformi ty 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>imental data have been available; the location of the point at which re­
</p>
<p>compression begins, the pressure at reattachment, and the peak pressure 
</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>

</p>
<p>step height. Nevertheless, the theory was able to predict the base pres­
</p>
<p>sure and the location of the point at which recompression begins quite 
</p>
<p>accurately. 
</p>

</p>
<p>values to force the theory ~ogive the correct base pressures for given 
</p>
<p>base bleed flowrates, and were not defined from detailed experimental 
</p>
<p>data in the recompression region. The fact that these N-factors do pre­
</p>
<p>dict the location of the start of recompression and the pressure level 
</p>
<p>at. reattachment lends support to the validity of. the theory.

<p>TEST DATA R~DUCTION AT AEDC

<p>The test configu:ration at AEDC has been designed to. provide sufficient data 
</p>
<p>for ev1:1,luation of. both full scale engine performance and vehicle drag. To 
</p>
<p>accomplish this, pressure profiles along t~e vehicle forebody and boattail, 
</p>
<p>and engine e,:cpansion surfaces will be measured in addition to flow engine 
</p>
<p>parameters and overall thrust. Model instrumentation is shown in Fig. B-1.

</p>
<p>. of each data point is presented in Taqle B-I. The con.stants used in the 
</p>
<p>equations of Table B-I are. presented .. in Table B-II. Items 1-7 indicate 
</p>
<p>average flow parameters for each run. Freestream flow conditions and.thrust 
</p>
<p>contributions from various: portions;. of' the vehicl.e and engine, are provided 
</p>
<p>by It~ms 8-22. Characteristic velocity of the primary and secondary streims 
</p>
<p>can .·be computed as indicated in I.terns· 23-26. Fliegner' s constant data for 
</p>
<p>air has been furnished to AEDC in separate cover. The engine throat Reynold's 
</p>
<p>.-
</p>
<p>number determined in Item 27 is used to obtain the primary throat area (Item 28) 
</p>
<p>from Rocketdyne calibration data presented in Fig. B-2. This will pr·ovide 
</p>
<p>a consistent comparison with AEDC. flow measurements by means of Item 29. 
</p>
<p>Pressure ratios calculated in Ite~s 30-32 form tni basis for obtaining the 
</p>
<p>thrust coefficients in Items 33 and 34. The relationship between pressure 
</p>
<p>ratio and thrust coefficient is shown in eqmi.tion below. The quantities in

<p>performance characteristics.
